Q65. GATE 2016 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let G be a CFG in CNF with n non-terminals, each generating exactly one terminal. The maximum height of a parse tree for a string of length k is:

Q70. GATE 2011 (1 Mark) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which of the following statements about CFLs is TRUE?

Q73. GATE 2015 Set 3 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which of the following problems is decidable for context-free grammars?

(A) Whether the language generated is empty
(B) Whether the language generated is regular
(C) Whether the language generated is finite
(D) Both (A) and (C)

Answer: (D)

Solution: For CFGs, emptiness is decidable (check if start symbol can derive a terminal string Ã¢â€ â€™ reduce to graph reachability). Finiteness is decidable (remove useless symbols, then check for cycles in the dependency graph). Regularity of a CFL is undecidable (Rice's theorem: "language is regular" is a non-trivial property of RE languages, and CFGs describe a subclass of RE).

Q74. GATE 2014 Set 2 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let G be a CFG in Chomsky Normal Form. The number of derivation steps to derive a string of length n is:

Q76. GATE 2017 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L be a recursively enumerable language. Which of the following is true?

(A) The complement of L is always recursively enumerable
(B) If L is recursive, then L is recursively enumerable
(C) If L is recursively enumerable, then L is recursive
(D) The complement of L is always recursive

Answer: (B)

Solution: Every recursive language is recursively enumerable (a decider is also a recognizer Ã¢â€ â€™ if it accepts, accept; if it rejects, halt and reject). (A) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ RE is not closed under complement. (C) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ there exist RE languages that are not recursive (e.g., halting problem). (D) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ complement of an RE language may not even be RE.

Q77. GATE 2016 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨MÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | M halts on empty input}. Which is true?

(D) L is not RE and not co-RE

Answer: (B)

Solution: L is the empty-input halting problem HÃƒÅ½Ã‚Âµ. We can recognize L: simulate M on ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Âµ and accept if M halts. But we cannot decide L (if M doesn't halt, we never know). HÃƒÅ½Ã‚Âµ is RE (enumerate all halting computations on empty input) but not recursive. Its complement (M does NOT halt on ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Âµ) is not even RE.

Q78. GATE 2015 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ A Turing Machine with a single tape that can only read (not write) is equivalent to:

(A) DFA
(B) NFA
(C) PDA
(D) LBA

Answer: (A)

Solution: A read-only TM has finite control and a tape with read-only head. Since the head cannot write, it can only move left and right while reading. This is equivalent to a two-way finite automaton, which is known to be equivalent to a DFA. Without writing, the TM gains no additional power over a DFA Ã¢â€ â€™ the set of reachable states for each prefix is finite and can be simulated by the subset construction.

Q79. GATE 2014 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which of the following about multi-tape TMs is TRUE?

(A) A k-tape TM is strictly more powerful than a single-tape TM
(B) A k-tape TM can be simulated by a single-tape TM with at most quadratic slowdown
(C) A k-tape TM can be simulated by a single-tape TM with at most exponential slowdown
(D) Nondeterministic TMs are strictly more powerful than deterministic TMs

Answer: (B)

Solution: A k-tape TM can be simulated by a single-tape TM by interleaving the k tapes onto one tape with markers. Each step of the k-tape TM requires scanning the entire single tape to read all k head positions, then scanning again to write. If the k-tape TM runs in t steps, the single-tape simulation runs in O(tÃƒâ€šÃ‚Â²) steps Ã¢â€ â€™ quadratic slowdown. (D) is false: nondeterministic TMs accept the same languages (RE) as deterministic TMs.

Q80. GATE 2013 (1 Mark) Ã¢â€ â€™ The Church-Turing thesis states that:

(A) Every function computable by a TM is computable by a finite automaton
(B) Every intuitively computable function is computable by a TM
(C) Every decision problem is decidable by a TM
(D) Every recursively enumerable language is recursive

Answer: (B)

Solution: The Church-Turing thesis claims that the informal notion of "effectively computable" coincides with TM computability. Any function that can be computed by an algorithm can be computed by a TM. It is a thesis (not a theorem) because "effectively computable" is informal. (A) is false (TMs are more powerful than FAs). (C) is false (undecidable problems exist). (D) is false (not all RE languages are recursive).

Q81. GATE 2012 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let U be a universal Turing Machine. The language accepted by U is:

(A) The set of all TM descriptions
(B) The set of all pairs ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© where M is a TM that accepts w
(C) The set of all halting TM descriptions
(D) The set of all recursively enumerable languages

Answer: (B)

Solution: A universal TM U takes an encoded pair ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© and simulates M on input w. U accepts ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© iff M accepts w. Therefore L(U) = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | M accepts w} = A_TM Ã¢â€ â€™ the acceptance language, which is RE but not recursive. (A) would include pairs where M does NOT accept w. (C) is different from the acceptance language.

Q82. GATE 2011 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L be a language over {0,1} accepted by a Turing Machine. Which is true?

(A) L is recursively enumerable
(B) L is recursive
(C) L is context-sensitive
(D) L is regular

Answer: (A)

Solution: By definition, the set of languages accepted by TMs is exactly the set of RE languages. L could be recursive, context-sensitive, or regular, but it must be at least RE. Not all RE languages are recursive Ã¢â€ â€™ the most general correct answer is (A).

Q83. GATE 2010 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Consider TM transition ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â´(q, a) = (q', b, L). What does this represent?

(A) In state q, reading a, write b, move left, go to q'
(B) In state q, reading a, read b, move left, go to q'
(C) In state q, reading a, erase b, move left, go to q'
(D) In state q, reading b, write a, move left, go to q'

Answer: (A)

Solution: Standard TM transition notation: ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â´(current_state, read_symbol) = (new_state, write_symbol, move_direction). So ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â´(q, a) = (q', b, L) means: if in state q and the tape head reads symbol a, write symbol b in the current cell, move the head one cell left (L), and transition to state q'. (B) misinterprets the write symbol as another read. (C) says "erase" instead of "write". (D) swaps the read and write symbols.

Q84. GATE 2016 Set 2 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� be recursive and LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ be recursively enumerable. Which is necessarily true?

(A) LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Â© LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ is recursive
(B) LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Âª LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ is recursively enumerable
(C) LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Å“ LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� is recursively enumerable
(D) Both (B) and (C)

Answer: (D)

Solution: (B) RE is closed under union. Since LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� is recursive (hence RE), LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Âª LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ is RE. (C) LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Å“ LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� = LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Â© LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â�^c. Since LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� is recursive, LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â�^c is recursive (hence RE). The intersection of two RE languages is RE. So LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â€šÂ¬Ã¢â‚¬Å“ LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� is RE. (A) is not necessarily true: LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� could be ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£* and LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ could be the halting problem. Then LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‚Â� ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Â© LÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ = HALT, which is RE but not recursive.

Undecidability & Reductions (8 Questions)​

Q85. GATE 2018 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which of the following about Rice's Theorem is TRUE?

(A) Rice's Theorem applies only to languages that are recursively enumerable
(B) Rice's Theorem states any non-trivial property of RE languages is undecidable
(C) Rice's Theorem can prove emptiness for CFGs is undecidable
(D) Rice's Theorem applies only to properties of regular languages

Answer: (B)

Solution: Rice's Theorem: any non-trivial property of the language of a TM (true for some RE languages and false for others) is undecidable. (A) is misleading Ã¢â€ â€™ Rice's theorem talks about properties of RE languages, but the TM whose language has the property must be considered. (C) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ emptiness for CFGs is decidable (Rice's theorem applies only to TM descriptions). (D) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ Rice's theorem applies to RE languages.

Q86. GATE 2017 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ The halting problem for Turing Machines is:

(A) Decidable
(B) RE but not decidable
(C) Co-RE but not decidable
(D) Neither RE nor co-RE

Answer: (B)

Solution: HALT_TM = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | M halts on input w}. We can recognize HALT: simulate M on w; if M halts, accept. But we cannot decide Ã¢â€ â€™ if M loops forever, we never know. HALT is RE (we can enumerate all halting computations) but not recursive (undecidable). Its complement (M does NOT halt) is not even RE. So HALT is RE but not recursive.

Q87. GATE 2016 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which problem about CFGs is UNDECIDABLE?

(A) Whether a given CFG generates the empty language
(B) Whether a given CFG generates an infinite language
(C) Whether a given CFG is ambiguous
(D) Whether a given CFG generates a finite language

Answer: (C)

Solution: Ambiguity of a CFG is undecidable Ã¢â€ â€™ there is no algorithm determining if a CFG has multiple parse trees for some string. Emptiness (A) and finiteness (B/D) are all decidable for CFGs. Ambiguity remains undecidable even for restricted grammar classes.

Q88. GATE 2015 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B denote many-one reducibility. Which is TRUE?

(A) If B is decidable and A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B, then A is decidable
(B) If A is undecidable and A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B, then B is undecidable
(C) If A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B and B ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ A, then A = B
(D) If A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B and B is RE, then A is recursive

Answer: (A)

Solution: Many-one reduction A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B means there exists a computable f such that w ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  A Ã¢â€¡â€� f(w) ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  B. If B is decidable, we decide A by computing f(w) and testing membership in B. (B) is the contrapositive and also valid, but the GATE answer focuses on (A) as the direct reduction property. (C) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ A and B could be different but equally hard. (D) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ A would be RE (not necessarily recursive).

Q89. GATE 2014 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ The Post Correspondence Problem (PCP) over ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£ is:

(A) Decidable for all ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£
(B) Undecidable for all ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£ with |ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£| ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¥ 2
(C) Decidable for |ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£| = 1
(D) Both (B) and (C)

Answer: (D)

Solution: PCP is undecidable in general (for |ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£| ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¥ 2). For |ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£| = 1 (unary alphabet), PCP is decidable Ã¢â€ â€™ it reduces to checking whether two sequences of natural numbers have matching prefix-sums, which is decidable. The undecidability proof for PCP uses a reduction from the halting problem and requires at least 2 symbols for encoding TM configurations.

Q90. GATE 2013 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨MÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | M is a TM that accepts at least one string}. Which is true?

(A) L is recursive
(B) L is RE but not recursive
(C) L is co-RE but not RE
(D) L is not RE and not co-RE

Answer: (B)

Solution: L = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨MÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | L(M) ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã¢â‚¬Â¦} Ã¢â€ â€™ the non-emptiness problem for TMs. This is RE: simulate M on all strings in parallel (dovetailing) and accept if any computation accepts. But it's not recursive: by Rice's theorem, "language is non-empty" is a non-trivial property of RE languages. However, it IS RE because we can search for a witness string.

Q91. GATE 2012 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let A be a known undecidable language. To prove B is undecidable:

(A) Reduce A to B (A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B)
(B) Reduce B to A (B ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ A)
(C) Show B is a subset of A
(D) Show A is a subset of B

Answer: (A)

Solution: To prove B is undecidable, reduce A (known undecidable) to B (A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B). If B were decidable, then A would be decidable via the reduction. Since A is known undecidable, B must also be undecidable. Direction matters: the reduction must go FROM the known hard problem TO the target. (B) would prove B is decidable if A is decidable Ã¢â€ â€™ useless when A is undecidable.

Q92. GATE 2011 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L = {ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨MÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© | L(M) is regular}. Which is true?

(A) L is decidable
(B) L is RE but not recursive
(C) L is co-RE but not RE
(D) L is not RE and not co-RE

Answer: (D)

Solution: "L(M) is regular" is a non-trivial property of the language of a TM. By Rice's theorem, it is undecidable. Furthermore, it is neither RE nor co-RE. Why? Regularity is a ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â°-complete property in the arithmetical hierarchy Ã¢â€ â€™ we can never confirm that a TM accepts exactly a regular language (would need to rule out all non-regular strings). It is strictly harder than the halting problem.

Complexity Classes (8 Questions)​

Q93. GATE 2018 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which of the following about P and NP is TRUE?

(A) P ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  NP is unknown
(B) NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  P is unknown
(C) P = NP implies every NP problem has a polynomial-time algorithm
(D) Every problem in NP is NP-complete

Answer: (C)

Solution: P ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  NP is known (every deterministic polynomial algorithm is also a non-deterministic one). NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  P is the P vs NP question (unknown). If P = NP, then every NP problem has a polynomial-time algorithm. (D) is false: problems in P are in NP but are not NP-complete (assuming P ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  NP), and if P = NP then all NP problems are in P but not necessarily NP-complete.

Q94. GATE 2017 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ A problem is NP-complete if:

(A) It is in NP and every problem in P reduces to it
(B) It is in NP and every problem in NP reduces to it
(C) It is in NP and it reduces to SAT
(D) It is in NP and SAT reduces to it

Answer: (B)

Solution: Definition: L is NP-complete if (1) L ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP, and (2) every L' ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP is polynomial-time reducible to L. (D) also implies NP-completeness (since SAT is NP-complete, SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã…Â¡ L makes L NP-hard, and L ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP makes it NP-complete). But (B) is the formal definition. GATE answer: (B).

Q95. GATE 2016 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which is TRUE about co-NP?

(A) co-NP = NP
(B) co-NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  NP
(C) P ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  co-NP
(D) co-NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  P

Answer: (C)

Solution: P is closed under complement. If a problem is in P, its complement is also in P, hence in NP, so the original problem is in co-NP. Thus P ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  co-NP. Whether co-NP = NP or co-NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  NP is unknown. co-NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  P is also unknown (and believed false).

Q96. GATE 2015 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ The Cook-Levin theorem states that:

(A) SAT is in P
(B) SAT is NP-complete
(C) 3-SAT is NP-complete
(D) Circuit-SAT is NP-complete

Answer: (B)

Solution: The Cook-Levin theorem (1971) proves SAT is NP-complete Ã¢â€ â€™ the first problem proven NP-complete. It shows every NP problem can be reduced to SAT in polynomial time by encoding a non-deterministic TM computation as a Boolean formula. (C) and (D) are corollaries proven later, not the Cook-Levin theorem itself.

Q97. GATE 2014 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let SPACE(s(n)) denote languages decidable by a TM using O(s(n)) space. Which is TRUE?

(A) SPACE(n) = NP
(B) SPACE(nÃƒâ€šÃ‚Â²) = PSPACE
(C) SPACE(n) ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  PSPACE
(D) NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  SPACE(n)

Answer: (C)

Solution: PSPACE = ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Âª_k SPACE(n^k). SPACE(n) (linear space) is a subset of PSPACE since n ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  O(n^k). (A) is an open problem. (B) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ SPACE(nÃƒâ€šÃ‚Â²) ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ PSPACE (proper subset). (D) is unknown Ã¢â€ â€™ NP could require more than linear space (current belief: NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Å¾ SPACE(n)).

Q98. GATE 2013 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let SAT be the Boolean satisfiability problem. Which is TRUE?

(A) SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P iff P = NP
(B) SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã¢â‚¬Â° P
(C) SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P
(D) SAT is not NP-complete

Answer: (A)

Solution: SAT is NP-complete (Cook-Levin). If SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P, then every NP problem reduces to SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P, so P = NP. Conversely, if P = NP, then SAT (which is in NP) is in P. So SAT ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P iff P = NP. (B) and (C) are open (P vs NP). (D) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ SAT was the first proven NP-complete problem.

Q99. GATE 2012 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Let L ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP. Which is necessarily true?

(A) L^c ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP
(B) L^c ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  co-NP
(C) L ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  P
(D) L is NP-complete

Answer: (B)

Solution: By definition, if L ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  NP, then L^c ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  co-NP. That is the definition of co-NP. (A) is unknown Ã¢â€ â€™ NP = co-NP is an open question. (C) is unknown (P vs NP). (D) is false Ã¢â€ â€™ not all NP problems are NP-complete.

Q100. GATE 2011 (2 Marks) Ã¢â€ â€™ Which is TRUE about PSPACE and NP?

(A) NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ PSPACE
(B) PSPACE ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ NP
(C) NP = PSPACE
(D) NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  PSPACE

Answer: (D)

Solution: NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  PSPACE: a polynomial-time NTM can be simulated by a TM using polynomial space Ã¢â€ â€™ we explore all computation paths one at a time, reusing space. Whether the inclusion is strict (NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Å¡ PSPACE) is unknown, though believed true. We can assert NP ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  PSPACE but cannot claim proper subset.

Common Traps, Tricks & Formula Cheat Sheet​

15 GATE-Specific Traps in TOC​

Trap 1: Confusing DCFL vs CFL Key distinction:

  • DCFL: accepted by a deterministic PDA (DPDA) Ã¢â€ â€™ at most one move per (state, input, stack-top)
  • NCFL: accepted by a non-deterministic PDA but NOT by any DPDA
  • Test: Can you accept with a single stack making deterministic decisions? If you need to "guess" (e.g., midpoint of wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿), it's NCFL.

Examples: {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ DCFL, {wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ NCFL (need to guess midpoint), {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â� | n ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  m} Ã¢â€ â€™ DCFL (complement of DCFL within ab), {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿ ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Âª aÃƒâ€šÃ‚Â²ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ NCFL.

Trap 2: Applying Pumping Lemma Incorrectly The pumping lemma is a necessary condition, not sufficient. If L fails pumping lemma Ã¢â€ â€™ L is NOT regular/CFL. If L passes Ã¢â€ â€™ L MIGHT still be non-regular/non-CFL.

Common mistakes:

  • Choosing wrong string (must be in L and long enough)
  • Not considering ALL possible divisions (you must show NO valid division works)
  • Forgetting |xy| ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ p (regular) or |vxy| ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ p (CFL) Ã¢â€ â€™ the pumping window constraint
  • Reversing quantifiers: YOU choose s, the LEMMA chooses the division

Trap 3: Reduction Direction To prove B undecidable: A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B (reduce known undecidable A TO B). Wrong: "B reduces to A" (B ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ A) Ã¢â€ â€™ this would prove B is decidable if A is decidable, useless when A is undecidable. Mnemonic: "A reduces to B" = "A is no harder than B" = B is at least as hard as A.

Trap 4: Rice's Theorem Applicability Applies ONLY to properties of the LANGUAGE of a TM, NOT the TM itself.

Applies (undecidable)Does NOT apply (may be decidable)
"L(M) is empty""M has exactly 5 states"
"L(M) is regular""M halts on input w"
"L(M) contains ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Âµ""M ever moves left on blank"
"L(M) is finite""M uses at most 100 tape cells"

Trap 5: Regular ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Â© CFL = CFL Intersection of a regular language and a CFL is ALWAYS a CFL. But intersection of two CFLs may NOT be a CFL. Example: {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿cÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�} ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Â© {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�cÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�} = {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿cÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ not CFL.

Trap 6: Complement of RE Languages If L is RE, L^c may or may not be RE.

  • L is recursive ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Âº both L and L^c are RE
  • L is RE but not recursive ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¹ L^c is NOT RE
  • This gives a rapid test: if you can show both L and L^c are RE, then L is recursive.

Trap 7: DFA Minimization Ã¢â€ â€™ Initial Marking The table-filling algorithm: initially mark ALL pairs (p, q) where p ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  F and q ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã¢â‚¬Â° F (or vice versa) as distinguishable. Many students forget edge cases involving unreachable states.

Trap 8: CFG Decision Problems

  • Emptiness: decidable (graph reachability)
  • Membership (word problem): decidable (CYK, O(nÃƒâ€šÃ‚Â³))
  • Finiteness: decidable (useless symbols + cycle check)
  • Ambiguity: UNDECIDABLE
  • Regularity: UNDECIDABLE
  • Equivalence: UNDECIDABLE

Trap 9: CSL vs Recursive All CSLs are recursive, but not all recursive languages are CSL. CSL is a proper subset of recursive languages. LBAs define CSLs.

Trap 10: Context-Free ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  Deterministic Not all CFLs are DCFLs. DCFL is a proper subset of CFL. {wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿} is the classic NCFL Ã¢â€ â€™ a DPDA cannot guess the midpoint.

Trap 11: "Recursive" vs "Recursively Enumerable"

  • Recursive (R) = decidable = TM halts on ALL inputs
  • RE = semi-decidable = TM accepts strings in L, may loop on strings not in L
  • Every recursive language is RE, but not vice versa
  • L is recursive iff both L and its complement are RE

Trap 12: P vs NP vs NP-Complete Not all NP problems are NP-complete. If P ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  NP, problems in P are in NP but not NP-complete. NP-complete requires both: (1) in NP, and (2) every NP problem reduces to it.

Trap 13: {ww} vs {wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿}

  • {ww} Ã¢â€ â€™ NOT context-free (needs to match symbol-by-symbol in order, impossible with single stack)
  • {wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ IS context-free (push w, pop wÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿), but NCFL (need non-determinism for midpoint)

Trap 14: Infinite Union of Regular Languages An infinite union of regular languages is NOT necessarily regular. Example: {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} = ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‚Âª_{kÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¥0} {aÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�}. Each {aÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�} is finite (regular), but the infinite union is the non-regular {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿}. Regular languages are only closed under FINITE union.

Trap 15: Homomorphic Images of CFLs CFLs are closed under homomorphism (including erasing homomorphisms). This means removing symbols via homomorphism preserves context-freeness. Regular languages are also closed under both homomorphism and inverse homomorphism.

Decision Procedure Table​

Key pattern: Everything decidable for Regular; emptiness/finiteness/membership decidable for CFL; only membership decidable for RE.

Key Reduction Examples for Undecidability Proofs​

1. A_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ HALT_TM Given ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â©, construct M': simulate M on w; if M accepts, halt; if M rejects, loop. Then ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  A_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Âº ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M'ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  HALT_TM. This shows HALT is undecidable given A_TM is undecidable.

2. HALT_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ EMPTY_TM Given ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â©, construct M': on any input x, simulate M on w. If M halts, accept x. Then L(M') = ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£* (if M halts on w) or L(M') = ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã¢â‚¬Â¦ (if M loops). So ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã¢â‚¬Â° HALT_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Âº ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M'ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  EMPTY_TM. This proves EMPTY_TM is undecidable.

3. A_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ REGULAR_TM Given ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â©, construct M': on input x, if x ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  {0ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿1ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} accept; otherwise simulate M on w and accept if M accepts w. Then L(M') = ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£* (regular) if M accepts w, else L(M') = {0ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿1ÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} (non-regular). So ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  A_TM ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Âº ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M'ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  REGULAR_TM.

4. PCP ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ CFG Ambiguity Given PCP instance (A,B) over ÃƒÅ½Ã‚Â£, construct CFG with productions generating all possible concatenations from both sequences. The CFG is ambiguous iff the PCP instance has a solution, proving CFG ambiguity undecidable.

Common Template: To prove B undecidable, reduce A ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¤ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Å¡Ã‹Å“ B where A is known undecidable. Construct f(ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M,wÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â©) = ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â¨M'ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Â© such that M' simulates M on w and uses the outcome to determine M''s language behavior. The reduction must be computable and correct (w ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  A ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â¸Ã‚Âº f(w) ÃƒÂ¢Ã‹â€ Ã‹â€  B).

Exam Time-Saving Insights​

  1. Quick Regularity Test for {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�}:

    • Condition on n or m independently Ã¢â€ â€™ regular (e.g., n even, m ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â¥ 5)
    • Condition relating n and m (n = m, n > m, n ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  m) Ã¢â€ â€™ NOT regular (CFL/DCFL)
    • Exception: "n = m or n ÃƒÂ¢Ã¢â‚¬Â°Ã‚Â  m" = all of ab Ã¢â€ â€™ regular

  2. Identify DCFL quickly:

    • Can you process left-to-right with deterministic stack decisions?
    • {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ DCFL (push a, pop b)
    • {wwÃƒÂ¡Ã‚Â´Ã‚Â¿} Ã¢â€ â€™ NCFL (need to guess midpoint)
    • {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�cÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ¢â‚¬â€œ | n = m} Ã¢â€ â€™ DCFL; {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�cÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ¢â‚¬â€œ | m = p} Ã¢â€ â€™ DCFL
    • {aÃƒÂ¢Ã‚Â�Ã‚Â¿bÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�cÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ¢â‚¬â€œ | n = m or m = p} Ã¢â€ â€™ NCFL (guess which condition)

  3. Rice's Theorem Quick Check:

    • Property of LANGUAGE? ÃƒÂ¢Ã…â€œÃ¢â‚¬Å“ Non-trivial? ÃƒÂ¢Ã…â€œÃ¢â‚¬Å“ Ã¢â€ â€™ undecidable
    • Property of TM itself? Ã¢â€ â€™ Rice's theorem does NOT apply

  4. CFL Closure Mnemonic:

    • Closed: Union, Concatenation, Kleene star, Reversal, Homomorphism Ã¢â€ â€™ "UCK-RH"
    • NOT closed: Intersection, Complement, Difference Ã¢â€ â€™ "ICD"

  5. P vs NP Quick Identification:

    • Verifiable in polynomial time? Ã¢â€ â€™ NP
    • Solvable in polynomial time? Ã¢â€ â€™ P
    • NP-complete problems: SAT, 3-SAT, VC, Clique, SubsetSum, HamCycle
    • P problems: Shortest Path, MST, Sorting, Matching, 2-SAT

  6. Language Classification Order: Given any language, determine its place in the hierarchy:

    1. Try DFA/NFA/RE Ã¢â€ â€™ regular?
    2. Try PDA/CFG Ã¢â€ â€™ CFL?
    3. Try LBA Ã¢â€ â€™ CSL?
    4. Try TM (always halts) Ã¢â€ â€™ recursive?
    5. Try TM (may loop) Ã¢â€ â€™ RE?
    6. Otherwise Ã¢â€ â€™ non-RE (use reductions)

  7. Diagonalization Core Idea: List all TMs and their behavior; construct a TM that differs on the diagonal. This proves the halting problem undecidable and is the foundation of all undecidability proofs.

  8. Savitch's Theorem: NSPACE(f(n)) ÃƒÂ¢Ã…Â Ã¢â‚¬Â  DSPACE(f(n)Ãƒâ€šÃ‚Â²). Corollary: PSPACE = NPSPACE. Non-deterministic space is at most quadratically more efficient than deterministic space.

  9. Regular Expression Counting: For r = (0|1)*0(0|1)ÃƒÂ¡Ã‚ÂµÃ‚Â�, strings of length n: count = 2^(n-k-1) Ãƒâ€šÃ‚Â· 1 Ãƒâ€šÃ‚Â· 2^k = 2^(n-1). The fixed symbol at position (n-k) from the end.

  10. Pumping Lemma String Selection:

    • For {aÃ¢Â�Â¿bÃ¢Â�Â¿}: choose s = aÃ¡Âµâ€“bÃ¡Âµâ€“
    • For {aÃ¢Â�Â¿bÃ¢Â�Â¿cÃ¢Â�Â¿}: choose s = aÃ¡Âµâ€“bÃ¡Âµâ€“cÃ¡Âµâ€“
    • For {ww}: choose s = 0Ã¡Âµâ€“1Ã¡Âµâ€“0Ã¡Âµâ€“1Ã¡Âµâ€“ (length 4p)
    • Key: ensure the pumping window (|vxy| Ã¢â€°Â¤ p) forces vy to stay within at most two adjacent symbol blocks

Summary​

Theory of Computation is a foundational GATE CS subject that explores the hierarchy of formal languages and the machines that recognize them. The Chomsky hierarchy Ã¢â‚¬â€� regular languages (recognized by DFAs/NFAs, expressed via regular expressions), context-free languages (recognized by PDAs, expressed via CFGs), context-sensitive languages (recognized by LBAs), and recursively enumerable languages (recognized by Turing machines) Ã¢â‚¬â€� forms the backbone of the subject. Key GATE topics include constructing DFAs and NFAs from regular expressions, designing PDAs for context-free languages, computing FIRST and FOLLOW sets for LL(1) parsing, applying the pumping lemma to prove non-regularity and non-context-freeness, and mastering reductions to prove undecidability. The halting problem is the canonical undecidable problem; its proof via diagonalization is a recurring GATE concept. With 1-3 questions worth 5-8 marks, this subject rewards conceptual clarity and precise machine construction skills.

flowchart TD
    A[Chomsky Hierarchy] --> B[Type 3: Regular]
    A --> C[Type 2: Context-Free]
    A --> D[Type 1: Context-Sensitive]
    A --> E[Type 0: Recursively Enumerable]
    B --> B1[DFA / NFA]
    B --> B2[Regular Expressions]
    C --> C1[PDA]
    C --> C2[CFG]
    D --> D1[LBA]
    E --> E1[Turing Machine]
    E1 --> F{Halting Problem?}
    F -->|Yes| G[Undecidable]
    F -->|No| H[Decidable]

TypeScript Implementations​

/**
 * FiniteAutomatonSimulator
 * --------------------------
 * Simulates a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) given its
 * transition table, start state, and accepting states. Can also
 * build a simple NFA with epsilon-closure computation.
 */
class FiniteAutomatonSimulator {
  private transitions: Map<string, Map<string, string>> = new Map();
  private epsilonClosure: Map<string, Set<string>> = new Map();

  constructor(
    private states: string[],
    private alphabet: string[],
    private startState: string,
    private acceptStates: Set<string>,
    transitionTable: [string, string, string][]
  ) {
    for (const state of states) {
      this.transitions.set(state, new Map());
    }
    for (const [from, symbol, to] of transitionTable) {
      this.transitions.get(from)!.set(symbol, to);
    }
  }

  /**
   * Process an input string and return whether it is accepted.
   */
  accepts(input: string): boolean {
    let current = this.startState;
    for (const symbol of input) {
      const next = this.transitions.get(current)?.get(symbol);
      if (!next) return false;
      current = next;
    }
    return this.acceptStates.has(current);
  }

  /**
   * Returns the full computation path for a given input.
   */
  trace(input: string): string[] {
    const path: string[] = [this.startState];
    let current = this.startState;
    for (const symbol of input) {
      const next = this.transitions.get(current)?.get(symbol);
      if (!next) break;
      current = next;
      path.push(current);
    }
    return path;
  }

  /**
   * Build an NFA-epsilon closure (sets of states).
   */
  static computeEpsilonClosure(
    states: string[],
    epsilonTransitions: [string, string][]
  ): Map<string, Set<string>> {
    const closure = new Map<string, Set<string>>();
    for (const state of states) {
      const visited = new Set<string>();
      const stack = [state];
      while (stack.length > 0) {
        const s = stack.pop()!;
        if (visited.has(s)) continue;
        visited.add(s);
        for (const [from, to] of epsilonTransitions) {
          if (from === s) stack.push(to);
        }
      }
      closure.set(state, visited);
    }
    return closure;
  }

  toString(): string {
    let result = `Start: ${this.startState}\nAccept: ${[...this.acceptStates].join(', ')}\n`;
    for (const [state, map] of this.transitions) {
      for (const [symbol, next] of map) {
        result += `  ÃŽÂ´(${state}, ${symbol}) = ${next}\n`;
      }
    }
    return result;
  }
}

// Example: DFA for strings ending with "01"
const dfa = new FiniteAutomatonSimulator(
  ['q0', 'q1', 'q2'],
  ['0', '1'],
  'q0',
  new Set(['q2']),
  [
    ['q0', '0', 'q1'],
    ['q0', '1', 'q0'],
    ['q1', '0', 'q1'],
    ['q1', '1', 'q2'],
    ['q2', '0', 'q1'],
    ['q2', '1', 'q0'],
  ]
);

console.log('Test string "0101":', dfa.accepts('0101') ? 'Ã¢Å“â€œ Accepted' : 'Ã¢Å“â€” Rejected');
console.log('Trace:', dfa.trace('0101').join(' Ã¢â€ â€™ '));

Exercises​

5 Practice Problems

Exercise 1: Construct a DFA

Design a DFA that accepts all strings over {0,1} that end with "101".

Solution: States: q0 (no match), q1 (last char 1), q2 (last two "10"), q3 (last three "101" Ã¢â‚¬â€� accept). Transitions: ÃŽÂ´(q0,0)=q0, ÃŽÂ´(q0,1)=q1, ÃŽÂ´(q1,0)=q2, ÃŽÂ´(q1,1)=q1, ÃŽÂ´(q2,0)=q0, ÃŽÂ´(q2,1)=q3, ÃŽÂ´(q3,0)=q2, ÃŽÂ´(q3,1)=q1. Accept: q3.

Exercise 2: Regular Expression

Find a regular expression for strings over {0,1} with an even number of 0's.

Solution: (1*01*01*)* + 1* Ã¢â‚¬â€� strings with zero or an even number of 0's where 1's can appear anywhere. More compact: (1*01*01*)* | 1* for zero 0's.

Exercise 3: CFG Design

Design a CFG for L = {aÃ¢Â�Â¿bÃ¡ÂµÂ� \| n Ã¢â€°Â  m}.

Solution: L = {aÃ¢Â�Â¿bÃ¡ÂµÂ� \| n > m} Ã¢Ë†Âª {aÃ¢Â�Â¿bÃ¡ÂµÂ� \| n < m}. S Ã¢â€ â€™ A \| B. A Ã¢â€ â€™ aA \| aAb \| a (for n > m). B Ã¢â€ â€™ bB \| aBb \| b (for n < m).

Exercise 4: Pumping Lemma

Prove L = {ww \| w Ã¢Ë†Ë† {0,1}*} is not regular using the pumping lemma.

Solution: Choose s = 0Ã¡Âµâ€“1Ã¡Âµâ€“0Ã¡Âµâ€“1Ã¡Âµâ€“. For any decomposition s = xyz with |xy| Ã¢â€°Â¤ p, y must be within the first block of 0's (all 0's). Pumping y gives 0Ã¡Âµâ€“Ã¢Â�ÂºÃ¡ÂµÂ�1Ã¡Âµâ€“0Ã¡Âµâ€“1Ã¡Âµâ€“ which is not of form ww. Contradiction. Hence L is not regular.

Exercise 5: Undecidability

Show that the problem "Does a TM accept the empty string?" is undecidable.

Solution: Reduce the halting problem: Given (Ã¢Å¸Â¨MÃ¢Å¸Â©, x), construct M' that first writes x on tape and then simulates M. M' rejects ÃŽÂµ. Then M accepts x iff M' accepts ÃŽÂµ (but M' rejects ÃŽÂµ). Actually, a cleaner reduction: Given (M, x), construct M' that ignores its input, writes x on tape, and simulates M. M' accepts ÃŽÂµ iff M accepts x. Since the halting problem (and acceptance problem) is undecidable, this problem is also undecidable.

End of Theory of Computation Ã¢â‚¬â€� GATE CS Preparation.
